```text
1	\section{Experiments}
2	\label{sec:experiments}
3	
4	\subsection{Experimental Setup}
5	\label{sec:setup}
6	
7	We study lead-lag analysis pitfalls in iterative training collapse using Pythia-410M \cite{biderman2023pythia}, a 410M-parameter decoder-only language model.
8	Pythia provides a controlled setting: small enough for multi-seed experiments across five contamination levels, yet large enough to exhibit meaningful generation-over-generation degradation.
9	Cross-architecture validation uses GPT-2-medium (345M parameters, also decoder-only), a second transformer family of similar scale; results are reported in \S\ref{sec:method_sensitivity}, \S\ref{sec:regime}, and \S\ref{sec:discussion}.
10	Training data is drawn from WikiText-103 \cite{merity2017pointer}, using 5{,}000-sample subsets per generation.
11	
12	Our protocol follows the replace paradigm of \citet{shumailov2024curse}, adapted for mixed synthetic-real training at $\alpha{<}1.0$.
13	Each generation $t$ proceeds in three steps: (1)~fine-tune the generation-$(t{-}1)$ model for 5{,}000 steps on a mixture of $\alpha$ fraction synthetic data and $(1{-}\alpha)$ fraction real data (AdamW optimizer, learning rate $5 \times 10^{-5}$, 500 warmup steps, weight decay $0.01$, batch size 4 for Pythia-410M, 32 for GPT-2-medium); (2)~generate 5{,}000 synthetic samples from the fine-tuned model (temperature $1.0$, top-$k{=}50$, max length 512 tokens); (3)~fully replace the synthetic pool.
14	We run $T{=}10$ generations (11 time points including the generation-0 baseline) across contamination levels $\alpha \in \{0.00, 0.25, 0.50, 0.75, 1.00\}$.
15	The $\alpha{=}1.0$ condition uses four random seeds $\{42, 43, 44, 45\}$; $\alpha{=}0.00$, $\alpha{=}0.25$, and $\alpha{=}0.75$ each use three seeds $\{42, 43, 44\}$; $\alpha{=}0.50$ uses three seeds $\{42, 43, 44\}$.
16	
17	We track two \emph{canary metrics} (cheap-to-compute statistics hypothesized to precede downstream degradation, inspired by early-warning signals in dynamical systems \cite{scheffer2009early}) and three \emph{downstream metrics}.
18	Canary metrics are token entropy (next-token prediction entropy averaged over held-out samples) and expected calibration error (ECE).
19	Downstream metrics are distinct-1 and distinct-2 ($n$-gram diversity \cite{li2016diversity}, computed over all 5{,}000 generated samples per generation) and perplexity (evaluated on held-out real data).
20	Token entropy is the mean next-token prediction entropy $H(p_\theta | \text{context})$ averaged over 1{,}000 held-out samples; ECE uses 15 equal-width bins.
21	We additionally evaluate effective rank and Self-BLEU as candidate canaries (\S\ref{sec:case_study}).
22	Results for distinct-3 are reported in Appendix~\ref{app:distinct3}.
23	Code and data will be released upon publication.
24	
25	All hypothesis tests apply Benjamini--Hochberg (BH) FDR correction at $q{=}0.05$.
26	Permutation Granger causality tests use 5{,}000 permutations on first-differenced series \cite{shojaie2022granger}.
27	Cross-correlation analysis uses a maximum lag of 5 generations.
28	Table~\ref{tab:drift_summary} summarizes raw metric drift from generation~0 to generation~10 across all five contamination levels.
29

48	
49	\subsection{Method Sensitivity Analysis}
50	\label{sec:method_sensitivity}
51	
52	We open with Claim~3 (method sensitivity) because it conditions the interpretation of all subsequent results.
53	If different statistical methods yield contradictory conclusions on the same data, any single-method analysis is unreliable by construction.
54	
55	\paragraph{Five-method ablation.}
56	We apply five lead-lag detection methods to the $\alpha{=}1.0$ condition (4~seeds, 16 canary$\to$downstream pairs).
57	The fraction of pairs reaching BH-corrected FDR significance varies dramatically by method: naive cross-correlation \textbf{16/16} (100\%), differenced cross-correlation \textbf{14/16} (87.5\%), threshold onset detection \textbf{9/16} (56.3\%), permutation Granger causality \textbf{6/16} (37.5\%), and Toda--Yamamoto Granger \textbf{4/16} (25.0\%).
58	Method choice alone swings significance from \textbf{100\% to 25\%}: 50--57\% of pairs classified as significant under one method flip to non-significant under another.
59	The spurious-signal rate (the fraction of pairs detected by the most liberal method but not confirmed by the most conservative) is \textbf{75\%} across all five methods and \textbf{62.5\%} when excluding Toda--Yamamoto, whose low statistical power at $T{=}10$ inflates this rate (\S\ref{sec:consensus_validation}).
60	Table~\ref{tab:method_comparison} reports significance rates restricted to 8 validated canary$\to$downstream pairs (excluding failed candidates); the 16-pair rates above include effective rank and Self-BLEU to capture the full method-sensitivity landscape.
61	
62	\paragraph{First-differencing diagnostic.}
63	A single preprocessing decision can eliminate an entire analysis channel.
64	For canary$\to$diversity pairs at $\alpha{=}1.0$, all pairs retain unanimous FDR significance after first-differencing (24/24$\to$24/24), confirming genuine temporal precedence rather than shared-trend confounding.
65	For canary$\to$perplexity, however, undifferenced tests find near-universal significance ($F{=}16$--$36$) while differenced tests find \textbf{zero} significant pairs ($F{\approx}0$, $p{=}0.99$; Figure~\ref{fig:differencing_dmax}a).
66	The apparent canary$\to$perplexity relationship is entirely an artifact of co-trending.
67	Notably, at $\alpha{=}0.75$, differenced cross-correlation finds 8/8 pairs significant in \emph{both} forward and reverse directions per seed, demonstrating that this method alone cannot distinguish genuine directional precedence from shared dynamics even after detrending.
68	
69	\paragraph{Augmentation order sensitivity.}
70	Toda--Yamamoto Granger tests show extreme sensitivity to the augmentation order $d_{\max}$ (Figure~\ref{fig:differencing_dmax}b).
71	At $d_{\max}{=}0$ (most permissive), approximately 6\% of pairs are rejected; at adaptive $d_{\max}$ determined by ADF unit-root tests, 75\%; at $d_{\max}{=}1$ (most conservative), \textbf{94\%}.
72	This single hyperparameter swings conclusions from near-universal significance to near-universal non-significance.
73	
74	\begin{figure}[t]
75	\centering
76	\includegraphics[width=\linewidth]{figures/differencing_dmax.pdf}
77	\caption{First-differencing diagnostic and augmentation order sensitivity (Pythia-410M, $\alpha{=}1.0$, 4~seeds). \textbf{(a)}~First-differencing eliminates the canary$\to$perplexity channel: undifferenced tests find near-universal significance ($F{=}16$--$36$) while differenced tests find zero significant pairs ($F{\approx}0$, $p{=}0.99$), exposing these associations as shared-trend artifacts. Canary$\to$diversity pairs retain significance after differencing (24/24$\to$24/24). \textbf{(b)}~Toda--Yamamoto Granger significance rates vary dramatically with augmentation order $d_{\max}$: from $\sim$6\% rejection at $d_{\max}{=}0$ to 94\% at $d_{\max}{=}1$, demonstrating that this single hyperparameter can reverse conclusions about temporal precedence.}
78	\label{fig:differencing_dmax}
79	\end{figure}
80	
81	\paragraph{Inter-method agreement.}
82	Cohen's $\kappa$, averaged over all method pairs, is \textbf{0.185} (near chance; poor agreement); this value is consistent across contamination levels ($\alpha{=}0.0$ through $\alpha{=}1.0$), indicating that method disagreement is a structural property of the analysis rather than a regime-specific artifact.
83	Cross-architecture validation on GPT-2-medium (12 canary$\to$downstream pairs) yields Fleiss' $\kappa{=}0.091$, also in the ``slight agreement'' range, confirming that low inter-method agreement is not architecture-specific.
84	Restricting to three informative methods (excluding naive cross-correlation and Toda--Yamamoto, which anchor the permissive and conservative extremes respectively) raises $\kappa$ to 0.667 (substantial agreement), indicating that the low full-panel $\kappa$ is driven by the deliberate inclusion of methods spanning the entire permissive-to-conservative spectrum rather than by fundamental incoherence among methods.
85	Table~\ref{tab:method_comparison} extends the analysis across contamination levels.
86	At $\alpha{=}0.0$ (no synthetic data, serving as null control), naive cross-correlation finds \textbf{100\%} of pairs significant, a textbook spurious-regression outcome in trending time series.
87	Permutation Granger correctly finds 0\% across all three null-control seeds; Toda--Yamamoto finds 0--12.5\% (1/24 total, attributable to a degenerate $\text{df}_{\text{resid}}{=}1$ case; \S\ref{sec:consensus_validation}).
88	At $\alpha{=}0.50$, naive and differenced cross-correlation both find 100\% significance while threshold onset and all causal methods find 0\% (Figure~\ref{fig:consensus_heatmap}), illustrating that even among non-causal methods, sensitivity diverges sharply at this boundary.
89	
90	\paragraph{Method$\times$regime interaction.}
91	Table~\ref{tab:method_comparison} reveals that the correlation-versus-causal divergence is not uniform across contamination levels.
92	At $\alpha{=}0.25$, differenced cross-correlation drops to 25\% and threshold onset to 0\%, while naive cross-correlation remains at 100\%, demonstrating that even partial detrending removes marginal associations at low contamination.
93	In the lowest-contamination regime ($\alpha{\leq}0.25$, three seeds), the false confirmation rate for the most liberal method approaches 100\%, while all Granger-type tests correctly find 0\%, a regime where single-method analysis is maximally dangerous.
94	The graded response across methods, combined with the block structure visible in Figure~\ref{fig:consensus_heatmap}, motivates the regime-dependent analysis in \S\ref{sec:regime}.
95	
96	\begin{figure}[t]

99	\caption{Method$\times$regime consensus heatmap (Pythia-410M). Each cell shows the consensus tier ($K$-of-$M$ agreement) for a given lead-lag method (rows) and contamination level $\alpha$ (columns). A clear block structure emerges: correlation-based methods (naive, differenced, threshold) find significance across all $\alpha$ levels, while Granger-type methods find significance only at $\alpha{\geq}0.75$. The $\alpha{=}0.50$ column is maximally dangerous (high correlation coexists with zero causal precedence), illustrating Pitfall~3 (regime dependence). Color scale: dark = significant (Tier~3), light = non-significant (Tier~1).}

101	\end{figure}

121	
122	\subsection{Regime-Dependent Detectability}
123	\label{sec:regime}
124	
125	Beyond method choice, the contamination level~$\alpha$ determines whether a temporal lead exists to detect.
126	We present regime effects at two levels: raw metric drift (method-independent) and Granger-precedence detectability.
127	
128	\paragraph{Raw drift.}
129	Table~\ref{tab:drift_summary} shows that diversity collapse occurs at \emph{all} contamination levels, not only under full synthetic replacement.
130	Distinct-1 declines by 13.8\% even at $\alpha{=}0.0$ (attributable to fine-tuning on repeated real-data subsets), and more steeply at intermediate contamination: $-40.3{\pm}0.3\%$ ($\alpha{=}0.25$), $-37.1{\pm}0.4\%$ ($\alpha{=}0.50$), $-32.3{\pm}0.5\%$ ($\alpha{=}0.75$), and $-27.8{\pm}1.6\%$ ($\alpha{=}1.0$).
131	The non-monotonic pattern (diversity declining \emph{more} at moderate~$\alpha$ than at extreme~$\alpha$) is confirmed by 3-seed replication at $\alpha{=}0.25$ (std${=}0.3\%$), indicating that this relationship between contamination level and collapse severity is robust rather than a seed-specific artifact.
132	
133	\begin{figure}[t]
134	\centering
135	\includegraphics[width=\linewidth]{figures/regime_map.pdf}
136	\caption{Detectability regime map across contamination levels (Pythia-410M). Permutation Granger FDR significance rate (solid line) and consensus tier (shaded regions) as a function of $\alpha$. The onset boundary lies at $\alpha{\approx}0.25$--$0.50$: at $\alpha{\leq}0.25$, all seeds agree on null (0/24); at $\alpha{=}0.50$, detectability is seed-dependent (0/8--6/8, 3~seeds, median 3/8); at $\alpha{=}0.75$, directional asymmetry emerges robustly (19/24 forward, 0/24 reverse, 3~seeds) but method consensus remains transitional (Tier~2--3); at $\alpha{=}1.0$, full Confirmatory consensus is achieved (24/24 forward, Tier~3, 4~seeds). Multi-seed replication: $\alpha{=}0.00$, $\alpha{=}0.25$, $\alpha{=}0.50$, $\alpha{=}0.75$: 3~seeds; $\alpha{=}1.0$: 4~seeds.}
137	\label{fig:regime_map}
138	\end{figure}
139	
140	\paragraph{Granger-precedence detectability.}
141	Despite ubiquitous drift, causal detectability is sharply regime-dependent (Figure~\ref{fig:regime_map}).
142	Permutation Granger tests (first-differenced, BH-corrected) find 0/24 pairs FDR-significant at $\alpha{=}0.00$ (3~seeds), 0/24 at $\alpha{=}0.25$ (3~seeds), 0/8--6/8 at $\alpha{=}0.50$ (seed-dependent; 3~seeds, median 3/8), and \textbf{6/8} at $\alpha{=}1.0$.
143	At $\alpha{=}0.75$ (3~seeds), permutation Granger finds 19/24 forward-direction pairs FDR-significant with 0/24 reverse, confirming directional asymmetry; however, method-level consensus varies by seed (Tier~3 pairs: 4, 3, and 1 across seeds), placing this regime in a transitional detection state.
144	Under Bonferroni correction, approximately 3/8 pairs per seed survive, consistent with the transitional nature of the $\alpha{=}0.75$ boundary where marginal signals are sensitive to correction stringency.
145	Within-$\alpha$ multi-seed analysis at $\alpha{=}1.0$ yields \textbf{24/24} canary$\to$diversity pairs FDR-significant across all four seeds, with 0/24 in the reverse direction, confirming temporal asymmetry.
146	The resulting dose-response pattern is monotonic: causal detectability is absent below $\alpha{=}0.25$, seed-dependent at $\alpha{=}0.50$, and robust above $\alpha{=}0.75$ (Figure~\ref{fig:regime_map}).
147	The onset boundary lies at $\alpha{\approx}0.25$--$0.50$, consistent with theoretical expectations about signal-to-noise limits in short time series~\cite{boettiger2012limits}.
148	At the extremes, the transition is unambiguous: 0/24 FDR-significant pairs at $\alpha{\leq}0.25$ (3~seeds) versus 24/24 at $\alpha{=}1.0$ (4~seeds, Tier~3 consensus).
149	Between these extremes, individual-seed stochasticity determines whether the consensus threshold is crossed; the data map a boundary region rather than a sharp phase transition.
150	Sensitivity analysis confirms that treatment/null separation is robust to FDR threshold choice ($q{=}0.01$: 0/24 null, 0/24 treatment at $\alpha{=}0.75$; $q{=}0.10$: 0/24 null, 24/24 treatment; Appendix~\ref{app:robustness}).
151	Cross-architecture validation on GPT-2-medium confirms this boundary: at $\alpha{=}0.25$, at most 1/8 pairs reach Confirmatory consensus ($K{\geq}4/5$), consistent with the null regime, despite substantially stronger canary drift (entropy $-32.8\%$ vs.\ Pythia $-4.7{\pm}0.5\%$), while at $\alpha{=}1.0$ all 6 canary$\to$diversity pairs reach $K{\geq}4/5$ (\S\ref{sec:discussion}).
152	Table~\ref{tab:cross_arch_regime} summarizes the regime map across both architectures.
153	At $\alpha{=}0.25$, GPT-2-medium yields 1/8 pairs at Tier~3 ($K{\geq}4/5$) and 7/8 at Tier~2 ($K{\geq}3/5$), consistent with Pythia's 0/8 Tier~3, 0/8 Tier~2 result at the same contamination level, placing both architectures in the null/low-detectability regime.
154	
155	\begin{table}[t]
156	\centering
157	\small
158	\caption{Cross-architecture regime map: lead-lag consensus tier and metric drift at $\alpha{=}0.25$ and $\alpha{=}1.0$. Tier~3: $K{\geq}4/5$ methods; Tier~2: $K{\geq}3/5$. 172	
173	\paragraph{Onset boundary at $\alpha{=}0.50$.}
174	The $\alpha{=}0.50$ regime provides particularly informative evidence.
175	Naive and differenced cross-correlation find 100\% significance while threshold onset finds 0\% (Table~\ref{tab:method_comparison}), and causal methods confirm emerging detectability in a majority of seeds: two of three seeds show directional asymmetry under permutation Granger (6/8 and 3/8 forward, 0/8 reverse), while one seed shows synchronous collapse (0/8).
176	The majority-signal outcome places $\alpha{=}0.50$ in a transitional regime where causal detectability is emerging but not yet robust, bridging the null regime ($\alpha{\leq}0.25$) and the consistently detectable regime ($\alpha{\geq}0.75$).
177	This seed dependence exemplifies our central argument: even within a single regime and method, stochastic variation in training determines whether the consensus threshold is crossed.
178	At this boundary, canary sensitivity is metric-dependent: token entropy fails to reach FDR significance in any seed ($p_{\text{fdr}}{\geq}0.086$), while ECE achieves significance in the two signal-positive seeds (s43, s44), identifying ECE as the more sensitive canary at the onset boundary.
179	Figure~\ref{fig:consensus_heatmap} visualizes this interaction across all method$\times\alpha$ combinations, revealing a clear block structure that separates correlation-based methods from causal methods as a function of regime.
180	
181	
182	\subsection{Case Study: Canary Prediction of Diversity Collapse}
183	\label{sec:case_study}
184	
185	Having established method sensitivity (Claim~3) and regime dependence (Claim~2), we present the conditions under which canary prediction succeeds (Claim~1).
186	We scope this claim precisely: canary metrics predict \emph{diversity collapse} (declining distinct-$n$), not model collapse broadly.
187	
188	\paragraph{Full synthetic replacement ($\alpha{=}1.0$, 4 seeds).}
189	Token entropy exhibits robust Granger-precedence over diversity collapse metrics, confirmed by 4 independent statistical methods (permutation Granger power${=}1.0$), 4 seeds, and cross-architecture replication on GPT-2-medium (Tier~3 consensus; \S\ref{sec:discussion}).
190	Cross-correlation peaks at lag$={+}1$ with $|r|{>}0.99$ (cross-seed standard deviation: 0.0), establishing a consistent one-generation lead.
191	Figure~\ref{fig:metric_trajectory} illustrates the one-generation offset between token entropy decline and distinct-1 decline, visible across all four seeds.
192	Permutation Granger tests (first-differenced) confirm all canary$\to$diversity pairs as FDR-significant across all four seeds, including distinct-3 (Appendix~\ref{app:distinct3}).
193	Reverse-direction tests (downstream$\to$canary) find \textbf{0/24} FDR-significant (all $p{>}0.49$), establishing complete directional asymmetry.
194	
195	\begin{figure}[t]
196	\centering
197	\includegraphics[width=\linewidth]{figures/metric_trajectory.pdf}
198	\caption{Metric trajectories across 10 generations at $\alpha{=}1.0$ (Pythia-410M, 4~seeds shown as individual traces). Token entropy (top, blue) drops sharply at generation~1, while distinct-1 (bottom, red) begins declining at generations~2--3, establishing a visible one-generation canary lead. The consistent lag across all four seeds confirms that the temporal offset is robust rather than seed-specific. Shaded bands show the inter-seed range. This visual pattern corresponds to the statistical finding of 24/24 forward-direction and 0/24 reverse-direction FDR-significant pairs.}
199	\label{fig:metric_trajectory}
200	\end{figure}
201	
202	\paragraph{High contamination ($\alpha{=}0.75$, 3 seeds).}
203	Directional asymmetry is robustly confirmed across three independent seeds (permutation Granger: 19/24 forward pairs FDR-significant, 0/24 reverse).
204	However, method-level consensus varies by seed (Tier~3 pairs: 4, 3, and 1 across seeds 42, 43, 44), placing $\alpha{=}0.75$ squarely in the transitional detection regime.
205	Differenced cross-correlation finds 8/8 pairs significant in \emph{both} forward and reverse directions at each seed, confirming that this method alone cannot distinguish genuine directional precedence from shared dynamics, a seed-level demonstration of Pitfall~1 (\S\ref{sec:pitfalls}).
206	
207	\paragraph{ECE as a conditional secondary canary.}
208	ECE's forward consensus matches token entropy (both achieve PermG\_fwd 6/8 across all conditions), but the reverse profile diverges sharply.
209	Across 7 validated conditions (3 architectures $\times$ 2 scales $\times$ 2 domains), token entropy reverse direction yields \textbf{0/28} FDR-significant pairs (consistent unidirectional causality), while ECE reverse direction yields \textbf{13/28} significant pairs, with 5/7 conditions containing at least one reverse-significant pair, all in the distinct$\to$ECE direction.
210	This bidirectional coupling is the core reason ECE is classified as a secondary canary: ECE and diversity exhibit mutual feedback, and the interpretability of ECE's causal direction is correspondingly limited.
211	This result also instantiates Claim~3 (method sensitivity): when only forward significance is considered, the two canary types appear equivalent; incorporating reverse-direction tests separates them. The choice of analysis dimension alters the canary classification itself.
212	
213	\paragraph{Failed canary candidates.}
214	Not all metrics serve as canaries.
215	Effective rank and Self-BLEU show no detectable lead-lag relationship with any downstream metric under any method or contamination level, and are excluded from the consensus framework.
216	Effective rank behavior is model-dependent in an unexpected way: in Pythia-410M, effective rank decreases during collapse (representational contraction), whereas in GPT-2-medium at $\alpha{=}0.25$ it \emph{increases} from 2.37 to 8.48 (+258\%).
217	The GPT-2-medium starting effective rank is near rank-2, suggesting the model's initial representation is already highly concentrated; iterative training disperses it rather than concentrating it further.
218	This opposite directionality across architectures precludes effective rank as a universal canary and motivates architecture-specific analysis (\S\ref{sec:discussion}).
219	
220	\paragraph{Consensus filtering.}
221	Of 16 canary$\to$downstream pairs tested at $\alpha{=}1.0$ (4 candidate canaries $\times$ 4 downstream metrics), \textbf{6 survive} the full consensus pipeline ($K{\geq}4/5$ methods, $S{\geq}3/4$ seeds): token entropy and ECE each predicting distinct-1 and distinct-2 (4 pairs; distinct-3 results in Appendix~\ref{app:distinct3} bring the total to~6).
222	The 10 eliminated pairs comprise all canary$\to$perplexity relationships (identified as spurious by the first-differencing diagnostic) and all effective-rank and Self-BLEU pairs (failed canaries).
223	The consensus framework reduces the naive 100\% significance rate to the 37.5\% rate supported by the most conservative individual methods (Figure~\ref{fig:consensus_workflow}).
224	
225	
226	\subsection{Consensus Framework Validation}
227	\label{sec:consensus_validation}
228	
229	We validate the consensus framework's calibration using built-in experimental controls.
230	
231	\paragraph{False positive rate.}
232	At $\alpha{=}0.0$ (three seeds, 24 total pairs), no synthetic data is injected; any detected lead-lag association is a false positive.
233	Individual-method FPR ranges from \textbf{100\%} (naive cross-correlation) through 37.5\% (differenced) and 20.8\% (threshold) down to \textbf{0\%} (permutation Granger: 0/24 across all three seeds).
234	The consensus framework at $K{\geq}4/5$ achieves \textbf{FPR${=}$0\%} (0/24 pairs, three independent seeds), matching the best individual methods while providing robustness against miscalibration of any single method (Table~\ref{tab:fpr_fnr}).
235	
236	\paragraph{False negative rate.}
237	At $\alpha{=}1.0$, we define 6 effect-size reference pairs (token entropy and ECE $\to$ distinct-1/2/3) using criteria independent of the consensus methods: drift ratio ${\geq}3\times$ the $\alpha{=}0.0$ baseline (entropy $11.93\times$, ECE $73.12\times$) and canary onset strictly preceding downstream onset by ${\geq}1$ generation (\S\ref{sec:calibration}).
238	FNR is calibrated on a 4-method panel (excluding differenced cross-correlation to avoid circularity): at $K{\geq}3/4$, $S{\geq}3/4$, all~6 are recovered (\textbf{FNR${=}$0\%}).
239	Toda--Yamamoto alone yields FNR${=}$33.3\% (2/6), missing ECE$\to$distinct-2 and ECE$\to$distinct-3 due to Type~II errors at $T{=}10$.
240	Both FPR and FNR are validated on the joint method${\times}$seed dimension (FPR: 3~seeds at $\alpha{=}0.0$; FNR: 4~seeds at $\alpha{=}1.0$), with cross-seed FPR variance negligible (all three seeds yield 0/8 robust pairs at $K{\geq}4/5$).
241	Table~\ref{tab:fpr_fnr} reports the calibration results.
242	Table~\ref{tab:single-method-errors} complements this analysis by showing the practical consequences of relying on any single method: naive cross-correlation produces false positives at every $\alpha$ including the null control, Toda--Yamamoto misses true signals at $\alpha{=}0.75$, while the consensus at $K{\geq}3$ yields the correct verdict across all five contamination levels.

302	
303	\paragraph{Bootstrap power analysis.}
304	To quantify detection power beyond binary FNR, we perform bootstrap power analysis ($B{=}1{,}000$ resamples from 4-seed data at $\alpha{=}1.0$).
305	Permutation Granger achieves power${=}1.0$ on genuine pairs (canary$\to$diversity) and power${=}0.0$ on spurious pairs (canary$\to$perplexity), providing clean separation.
306	Toda--Yamamoto achieves power${=}0.05$--$0.20$ on genuine pairs (depending on $d_{\max}$), confirming its poor sensitivity at $T{=}10$.
307	We note that bootstrap power${=}1.0$ reflects the effect size in our 4-seed data and should not be interpreted as a universal power guarantee; furthermore, the two methods use different data-generating processes for calibration (permutation Granger resamples observed $\alpha{=}1.0$ trajectories, while Toda--Yamamoto power was estimated under a generic VAR(2) simulation with $\beta{=}0.3$).

330	
331	\subsection{Cross-Architecture, Cross-Scale, and Cross-Domain Generalization}
332	\label{sec:generalization}
333	
334	The methodological pitfalls and consensus framework developed above are calibrated on a single model family (Pythia-410M, WikiText-103).
335	To test whether these findings transfer, we evaluate the consensus pipeline along three generalization axes: architecture family (GPT-2-medium 345M, OPT-1.3B), model scale (Pythia-1.4B, $3.4\times$ parameters within the same family), and training domain (Pythia-1B trained on C4 rather than WikiText-103).
336	All conditions use $\alpha{=}1.0$ and a single seed.
337	
338	\begin{table}[t]
339	\centering
340	\small
341	\caption{Generalization of lead-lag detectability across architecture, scale, and domain ($\alpha{=}1.0$, single seed per condition). Each cell reports the fraction of canary$\to$downstream pairs reaching BH-corrected FDR significance ($q{=}0.05$): 8~pairs for Pythia models and OPT-1.3B, 6~pairs for GPT-2-medium (MAUVE excluded). \textbf{[TBD]}: experiments in progress.}
342	\label{tab:generalization}
343	\begin{tabular}{llccccccc}
344	\toprule
345	Variation & Model / Dataset & \rotatebox{70}{Naive} & \rotatebox{70}{Diff.\ XCorr} & \rotatebox{70}{Threshold} & \rotatebox{70}{PermG (fwd)} & \rotatebox{70}{PermG (rev)} & \rotatebox{70}{TY} & \rotatebox{70}{$K{\geq}4/5$} \\
346	\midrule
347	Baseline      & Pythia-410M / Wiki    & 7/8 & 6/8 & 6/8 & 6/8 & 0/8 & 6/8 & 6/8 \\
348	Architecture  & GPT-2-medium / Wiki   & 6/6 & 6/6 & 6/6 & 6/6 & 0/6 & 0/6 & 6/6 \\
349	Architecture  & OPT-1.3B / Wiki       & 6/8 & 6/8 & 8/8 & 6/8 & 0/8 & 2/8 & 6/8 \\
350	Scale ($3.4\times$) & Pythia-1.4B / Wiki & \multicolumn{7}{c}{\textbf{[TBD]}} \\
351	Domain        & Pythia-1B / C4        & \multicolumn{7}{c}{\textbf{[TBD]}} \\
352	\bottomrule
353	\end{tabular}
354	\end{table}
355	
356	\paragraph{Architecture generalization.}
357	Table~\ref{tab:generalization} confirms that the entropy-leads-diversity pattern generalizes across all three decoder-only transformer families tested to date.
358	All models reach Tier~3 consensus ($K{\geq}4/5$) with 6/8 (or 6/6) canary$\to$diversity pairs confirmed.
359	Permutation Granger directionality is perfect across architectures: all forward-direction pairs are FDR-significant while 0 reverse-direction pairs reach significance in any architecture.
360	
361	Method-level variation across architectures is informative.
362	Threshold onset detection reaches 8/8 on OPT-1.3B (the highest single-method score) because OPT's token entropy exhibits a pronounced generation-0$\to$1 discontinuity that cleanly exceeds the onset threshold.
363	Toda--Yamamoto ranges from 0/6 (GPT-2-medium) to 6/8 (Pythia-410M), consistent with its known sensitivity to residual degrees of freedom at short series lengths (\S\ref{sec:consensus_validation}).
364	Despite this cross-method variance, the consensus threshold absorbs it: all three architectures converge at $K{\geq}4/5$.
365	
366	\paragraph{Cross-condition patterns.}
367	Two structural patterns emerge across all generalization conditions.
368	First, canary$\to$perplexity pairs yield 0/14 at $K{\geq}4/5$ consensus across all conditions in Table~\ref{tab:generalization}, confirming that the spurious-channel finding from \S\ref{sec:method_sensitivity} is not architecture-specific but a structural property of the canary$\to$perplexity relationship: perplexity co-moves synchronously with canary metrics rather than lagging them, regardless of model family or training domain.
369	This elevates the perplexity channel result from a single-condition diagnostic (\S\ref{sec:case_study}) to a cross-validated finding: diversity collapse is the predictable downstream dimension, perplexity is not.
370	Second, restricting to canary$\to$diversity pairs (excluding the structurally non-predictable perplexity channel), 41 of 42 pairs across all conditions achieve $K{\geq}4/5$ consensus (97.6\%), with the sole exception in the Pythia-1.4B seed=42 condition.
371	This near-perfect diversity-specific consensus validates that the framework reliably identifies the predictable dimension of iterative training collapse across architectures, scales, and domains.

$+37.6{\\pm}14$ & $-14.1{\\pm}0.4$ & $-9.6{\\pm}0.4$ & $-20.6{\\pm}1.0$ \\\\\n0.25 & $-4.7{\\pm}0.5$ & $+81.8{\\pm}15.9$ & $-40.3{\\pm}0.3$ & $-56.4{\\pm}0.7$ & $-18.1{\\pm}0.8$ \\\\\n0.50 & $-18.0{\\pm}0.5$ & $+822.6{\\pm}18.3$ & $-37.1{\\pm}0.4$ & $-50.4{\\pm}1.0$ & $+21.0{\\pm}0.1$ \\\\\n0.75 & $-27.6{\\pm}0.2$ & $+1791{\\pm}18$ & $-32.3{\\pm}0.5$ & $-41.0{\\pm}1.0$ & $+182.6{\\pm}0.7$ \\\\\n1.00 & $-60.8{\\pm}1.2$ & $+3780{\\pm}30$ & $-27.8{\\pm}1.6$ & $-34.0{\\pm}3.3$ & $+3674{\\pm}45$ \\\\\n\\bottomrule\n\\end{tabular}\n\\end{table}\n\n\n\\subsection{Method Sensitivity Analysis}\n\\label{sec:method_sensitivity}\n\nWe open with Claim~3 (method sensitivity) because it conditions the interpretation of all subsequent results.\nIf different statistical methods yield contradictory conclusions on the same data, any single-method analysis is unreliable by construction.\n\n\\paragraph{Five-method ablation.}\nWe apply five lead-lag detection methods to the $\\alpha{=}1.0$ condition (4~seeds, 16 canary$\\to$downstream pairs).\nThe fraction of pairs reaching BH-corrected FDR significance varies dramatically by method: naive cross-correlation \\textbf{16/16} (100\\%), differenced cross-correlation \\textbf{14/16} (87.5\\%), threshold onset detection \\textbf{9/16} (56.3\\%), permutation Granger causality \\textbf{6/16} (37.5\\%), and Toda--Yamamoto Granger \\textbf{4/16} (25.0\\%).\nMethod choice alone swings significance from \\textbf{100\\% to 25\\%}: 50--57\\% of pairs classified as significant under one method flip to non-significant under another.\nThe spurious-signal rate (the fraction of pairs detected by the most liberal method but not confirmed by the most conservative) is \\textbf{75\\%} across all five methods and \\textbf{62.5\\%} when excluding Toda--Yamamoto, whose low statistical power at $T{=}10$ inflates this rate (\\S\\ref{sec:consensus_validation}).\nTable~\\ref{tab:method_comparison} reports significance rates restricted to 8 validated canary$\\to$downstream pairs (excluding failed candidates); the 16-pair rates above include effective rank and Self-BLEU to capture the full method-sensitivity landscape.\n\n\\paragraph{First-differencing diagnostic.}\nA single preprocessing decision can eliminate an entire analysis channel.\nFor canary$\\to$diversity pairs at $\\alpha{=}1.0$, all pairs retain unanimous FDR significance after first-differencing (24/24$\\to$24/24), confirming genuine temporal precedence rather than shared-trend confounding.\nFor canary$\\to$perplexity, however, undifferenced tests find near-universal significance ($F{=}16$--$36$) while differenced tests find \\textbf{zero} significant pairs ($F{\\approx}0$, $p{=}0.99$; Figure~\\ref{fig:differencing_dmax}a).\nThe apparent canary$\\to$perplexity relationship is entirely an artifact of co-trending.\nNotably, at $\\alpha{=}0.75$, differenced cross-correlation finds 8/8 pairs significant in \\emph{both} forward and reverse directions per seed, demonstrating that this method alone cannot distinguish genuine directional precedence from shared dynamics even after detrending.\n\n\\paragraph{Augmentation order sensitivity.}\nToda--Yamamoto Granger tests show extreme sensitivity to the augmentation order $d_{\\max}$ (Figure~\\ref{fig:differencing_dmax}b).\nAt $d_{\\max}{=}0$ (most permissive), approximately 6\\% of pairs are rejected; at adaptive $d_{\\max}$ determined by ADF unit-root tests, 75\\%; at $d_{\\max}{=}1$ (most conservative), \\textbf{94\\%}.\nThis single hyperparameter swings conclusions from near-universal significance to near-universal non-significance.\n\n\\begin{figure}[t]\n\\centering\n\\includegraphics[width=\\linewidth]{figures/differencing_dmax.pdf}\n\\caption{First-differencing diagnostic and augmentation order sensitivity (Pythia-410M, $\\alpha{=}1.0$, 4~seeds). \\textbf{(a)}~First-differencing eliminates the canary$\\to$perplexity channel: undifferenced tests find near-universal significance ($F{=}16$--$36$) while differenced tests find zero significant pairs ($F{\\approx}0$, $p{=}0.99$), exposing these associations as shared-trend artifacts. Canary$\\to$diversity pairs retain significance after differencing (24/24$\\to$24/24). \\textbf{(b)}~Toda--Yamamoto Granger significance rates vary dramatically with augmentation order $d_{\\max}$: from $\\sim$6\\% rejection at $d_{\\max}{=}0$ to 94\\% at $d_{\\max}{=}1$, demonstrating that this single hyperparameter can reverse conclusions about temporal precedence.}\n\\label{fig:differencing_dmax}\n\\end{figure}\n\n\\paragraph{Inter-method agreement.}\nCohen's $\\kappa$, averaged over all method pairs, is \\textbf{0.185} (near chance; poor agreement); this value is consistent across contamination levels ($\\alpha{=}0.0$ through $\\alpha{=}1.0$), indicating that method disagreement is a structural property of the analysis rather than a regime-specific artifact.\nCross-architecture validation on GPT-2-medium (12 canary$\\to$downstream pairs) yields Fleiss' $\\kappa{=}0.091$, also in the ``slight agreement'' range, confirming that low inter-method agreement is not architecture-specific.\nRestricting to three informative methods (excluding naive cross-correlation and Toda--Yamamoto, which anchor the permissive and conservative extremes respectively) raises $\\kappa$ to 0.667 (substantial agreement), indicating that the low full-panel $\\kappa$ is driven by the deliberate inclusion of methods spanning the entire permissive-to-conservative spectrum rather than by fundamental incoherence among methods.\nTable~\\ref{tab:method_comparison} extends the analysis across contamination levels.\nAt $\\alpha{=}0.0$ (no synthetic data, serving as null control), naive cross-correlation finds \\textbf{100\\%} of pairs significant, a textbook spurious-regression outcome in trending time series.\nPermutation Granger correctly finds 0\\% across all three null-control seeds; Toda--Yamamoto finds 0--12.5\\% (1/24 total, attributable to a degenerate $\\text{df}_{\\text{resid}}{=}1$ case; \\S\\ref{sec:consensus_validation}).\nAt $\\alpha{=}0.50$, naive and differenced cross-correlation both find 100\\% significance while threshold onset and all causal methods find 0\\% (Figure~\\ref{fig:consensus_heatmap}), illustrating that even among non-causal methods, sensitivity diverges sharply at this boundary.\n\n\\paragraph{Method$\\times$regime interaction.}\nTable~\\ref{tab:method_comparison} reveals that the correlation-versus-causal divergence is not uniform across contamination levels.\nAt $\\alpha{=}0.25$, differenced cross-correlation drops to 25\\% and threshold onset to 0\\%, while naive cross-correlation remains at 100\\%, demonstrating that even partial detrending removes marginal associations at low contamination.\nIn the lowest-contamination regime ($\\alpha{\\leq}0.25$, three seeds), the false confirmation rate for the most liberal method approaches 100\\%, while all Granger-type tests correctly find 0\\%, a regime where single-method analysis is maximally dangerous.\nThe graded response across methods, combined with the block structure visible in Figure~\\ref{fig:consensus_heatmap}, motivates the regime-dependent analysis in \\S\\ref{sec:regime}.\n\n\\begin{figure}[t]\n\\centering\n\\includegraphics[width=\\linewidth]{figures/consensus_heatmap.pdf}\n\\caption{Method$\\times$regime consensus heatmap (Pythia-410M). Each cell shows the consensus tier ($K$-of-$M$ agreement) for a given lead-lag method (rows) and contamination level $\\alpha$ (columns). A clear block structure emerges: correlation-based methods (naive, differenced, threshold) find significance across all $\\alpha$ levels, while Granger-type methods find significance only at $\\alpha{\\geq}0.75$. The $\\alpha{=}0.50$ column is maximally dangerous (high correlation coexists with zero causal precedence), illustrating Pitfall~3 (regime dependence). Color scale: dark = significant (Tier~3), light = non-significant (Tier~1).}\n\\label{fig:consensus_heatmap}\n\\end{figure}\n\n\\begin{table}[t]\n\\centering\n\\small\n\\caption{FDR significance rates (\\%) for five lead-lag methods across contamination levels (Pythia-410M). Method choice swings significance from 100\\% (naive cross-correlation) to 0\\% (permutation Granger) at $\\alpha{\\leq}0.25$, with seed-dependent onset at $\\alpha{=}0.50$, demonstrating that single-method conclusions are unreliable. Each cell reports the fraction of 8 canary$\\to$downstream pairs (token entropy and ECE $\\times$ 4 downstream metrics) reaching BH-corrected significance ($q{=}0.05$), excluding failed candidates (\\S\\ref{sec:case_study}). Seed coverage: $\\alpha{=}1.0$: 4 seeds (32 pairs); $\\alpha{=}0.00$, $\\alpha{=}0.75$: 3 seeds (24 pairs); $\\alpha{=}0.25$ Toda--Yamamoto: 3 seeds (24 pairs), other methods single seed; $\\alpha{=}0.50$: 3~seeds for permutation Granger (range shown), single seed for other methods. $^\\dagger$Seed-dependent onset: s42${=}$0/8, s43${=}$6/8, s44${=}$3/8 (median 3/8).}\n\\label{tab:method_comparison}\n\\begin{tabular}{lcccccc}\n\\toprule\nMethod & $\\alpha{=}0.0$ & $\\alpha{=}0.25$ & $\\alpha{=}0.50$ & $\\alpha{=}0.75$ & $\\alpha{=}1.0$ & FPR$_\\text{null}$ \\\\\n\\midrule\nNaive XCorr     & 100   & 100  & 100  & 100   & 100   & 100  \\\\\nDiff.\\ XCorr   & 37.5  & 25   & 100  & 100   & 100   & 37.5 \\\\\nThreshold       & 20.8  & 0    & 0    & 33.3  & 100   & 20.8 \\\\\nPerm.\\ Granger  & 0     & 0    & 0--75$^\\dagger$  & 79.2  & 75    & 0    \\\\\nToda--Yamamoto  & 0     & 12.5 & 0    & 12.5  & 59.4  & 0    \\\\\n\\bottomrule\n\\end{tabular}\n\\end{table}\n\n\n\\subsection{Regime-Dependent Detectability}\n\\label{sec:regime}\n\nBeyond method choice, the contamination level~$\\alpha$ determines whether a temporal lead exists to detect.\nWe present regime effects at two levels: raw metric drift (method-independent) and Granger-precedence detectability.\n\n\\paragraph{Raw drift.}\nTable~\\ref{tab:drift_summary} shows that diversity collapse occurs at \\emph{all} contamination levels, not only under full synthetic replacement.\nDistinct-1 declines by 13.8\\% even at $\\alpha{=}0.0$ (attributable to fine-tuning on repeated real-data subsets), and more steeply at intermediate contamination: $-40.3{\\pm}0.3\\%$ ($\\alpha{=}0.25$), $-37.1{\\pm}0.4\\%$ ($\\alpha{=}0.50$), $-32.3{\\pm}0.5\\%$ ($\\alpha{=}0.75$), and $-27.8{\\pm}1.6\\%$ ($\\alpha{=}1.0$).\nThe non-monotonic pattern (diversity declining \\emph{more} at moderate~$\\alpha$ than at extreme~$\\alpha$) is confirmed by 3-seed replication at $\\alpha{=}0.25$ (std${=}0.3\\%$), indicating that this relationship between contamination level and collapse severity is robust rather than a seed-specific artifact.\n\n\\begin{figure}[t]\n\\centering\n\\includegraphics[width=\\linewidth]{figures/regime_map.pdf}\n\\caption{Detectability regime map across contamination levels (Pythia-410M). Permutation Granger FDR significance rate (solid line) and consensus tier (shaded regions) as a function of $\\alpha$. The onset boundary lies at $\\alpha{\\approx}0.25$--$0.50$: at $\\alpha{\\leq}0.25$, all seeds agree on null (0/24); at $\\alpha{=}0.50$, detectability is seed-dependent (0/8--6/8, 3~seeds, median 3/8); at $\\alpha{=}0.75$, directional asymmetry emerges robustly (19/24 forward, 0/24 reverse, 3~seeds) but method consensus remains transitional (Tier~2--3); at $\\alpha{=}1.0$, full Confirmatory consensus is achieved (24/24 forward, Tier~3, 4~seeds). Multi-seed replication: $\\alpha{=}0.00$, $\\alpha{=}0.25$, $\\alpha{=}0.50$, $\\alpha{=}0.75$: 3~seeds; $\\alpha{=}1.0$: 4~seeds.}\n\\label{fig:regime_map}\n\\end{figure}\n\n\\paragraph{Granger-precedence detectability.}\nDespite ubiquitous drift, causal detectability is sharply regime-dependent (Figure~\\ref{fig:regime_map}).\nPermutation Granger tests (first-differenced, BH-corrected) find 0/24 pairs FDR-significant at $\\alpha{=}0.00$ (3~seeds), 0/24 at $\\alpha{=}0.25$ (3~seeds), 0/8--6/8 at $\\alpha{=}0.50$ (seed-dependent; 3~seeds, median 3/8), and \\textbf{6/8} at $\\alpha{=}1.0$.\nAt $\\alpha{=}0.75$ (3~seeds), permutation Granger finds 19/24 forward-direction pairs FDR-significant with 0/24 reverse, confirming directional asymmetry; however, method-level consensus varies by seed (Tier~3 pairs: 4, 3, and 1 across seeds), placing this regime in a transitional detection state.\nUnder Bonferroni correction, approximately 3/8 pairs per seed survive, consistent with the transitional nature of the $\\alpha{=}0.75$ boundary where marginal signals are sensitive to correction stringency.\nWithin-$\\alpha$ multi-seed analysis at $\\alpha{=}1.0$ yields \\textbf{24/24} canary$\\to$diversity pairs FDR-significant across all four seeds, with 0/24 in the reverse direction, confirming temporal asymmetry.\nThe resulting dose-response pattern is monotonic: causal detectability is absent below $\\alpha{=}0.25$, seed-dependent at $\\alpha{=}0.50$, and robust above $\\alpha{=}0.75$ (Figure~\\ref{fig:regime_map}).\nThe onset boundary lies at $\\alpha{\\approx}0.25$--$0.50$, consistent with theoretical expectations about signal-to-noise limits in short time series~\\cite{boettiger2012limits}.\nAt the extremes, the transition is unambiguous: 0/24 FDR-significant pairs at $\\alpha{\\leq}0.25$ (3~seeds) versus 24/24 at $\\alpha{=}1.0$ (4~seeds, Tier~3 consensus).\nBetween these extremes, individual-seed stochasticity determines whether the consensus threshold is crossed; the data map a boundary region rather than a sharp phase transition.\nSensitivity analysis confirms that treatment/null separation is robust to FDR threshold choice ($q{=}0.01$: 0/24 null, 0/24 treatment at $\\alpha{=}0.75$; $q{=}0.10$: 0/24 null, 24/24 treatment; Appendix~\\ref{app:robustness}).\nCross-architecture validation on GPT-2-medium confirms this boundary: at $\\alpha{=}0.25$, at most 1/8 pairs reach Confirmatory consensus ($K{\\geq}4/5$), consistent with the null regime, despite substantially stronger canary drift (entropy $-32.8\\%$ vs.\\ Pythia $-4.7{\\pm}0.5\\%$), while at $\\alpha{=}1.0$ all 6 canary$\\to$diversity pairs reach $K{\\geq}4/5$ (\\S\\ref{sec:discussion}).\nTable~\\ref{tab:cross_arch_regime} summarizes the regime map across both architectures.\nAt $\\alpha{=}0.25$, GPT-2-medium yields 1/8 pairs at Tier~3 ($K{\\geq}4/5$) and 7/8 at Tier~2 ($K{\\geq}3/5$), consistent with Pythia's 0/8 Tier~3, 0/8 Tier~2 result at the same contamination level, placing both architectures in the null/low-detectability regime.\n\n\\begin{table}[t]\n\\centering\n\\small\n\\caption{Cross-architecture regime map: lead-lag consensus tier and metric drift at $\\alpha{=}0.25$ and $\\alpha{=}1.0$. Tier~3: $K{\\geq}4/5$ methods; Tier~2: $K{\\geq}3/5$. Drift is generation~0$\\to$10. GPT-2-medium $\\alpha{=}0.25$: single seed (seed=42); Pythia $\\alpha{=}0.25$: 3-seed mean${\\pm}$std. The onset boundary ($\\alpha{\\approx}0.25$--$0.50$) is consistent across two transformer decoder-only model families of similar scale.}\n\\label{tab:cross_arch_regime}\n\\begin{tabular}{llcccccc}\n\\toprule\n$\\alpha$ & Model & Entropy & ECE & Distinct-1 & Distinct-2 & Perm.\\ Granger & Tier~3 \\\\\n\\midrule\n0.25 & Pythia-410M  & $-4.7{\\pm}0.5$ & $+81.8{\\pm}15.9$ & $-40.3{\\pm}0.3$ & $-56.4{\\pm}0.7$ & 0/8 & 0/8 \\\\\n0.25 & GPT-2-medium & $-32.8$ & $+422$ & $-23.5$ & $-31.6$ & 0/8 & 1/8 \\\\\n\\midrule\n1.00 & Pythia-410M  & $-60.8{\\pm}1.2$ & $+3780{\\pm}30$ & $-27.8{\\pm}1.6$ & $-34.0{\\pm}3.3$ & 6/8 & 6/6 \\\\\n1.00 & GPT-2-medium & \\multicolumn{6}{c}{(reported in \\S\\ref{sec:discussion}; all 6 canary$\\to$diversity Tier~3)} \\\\\n\\bottomrule\n\\end{tabular}\n\\end{table}\n\n\\paragraph{Onset boundary at $\\alpha{=}0.50$.}\nThe $\\alpha{=}0.50$ regime provides particularly informative evidence.\nNaive and differenced cross-correlation find 100\\% significance while threshold onset finds 0\\% (Table~\\ref{tab:method_comparison}), and causal methods confirm emerging detectability in a majority of seeds: two of three seeds show directional asymmetry under permutation Granger (6/8 and 3/8 forward, 0/8 reverse), while one seed shows synchronous collapse (0/8).\nThe majority-signal outcome places $\\alpha{=}0.50$ in a transitional regime where causal detectability is emerging but not yet robust, bridging the null regime ($\\alpha{\\leq}0.25$) and the consistently detectable regime ($\\alpha{\\geq}0.75$).\nThis seed dependence exemplifies our central argument: even within a single regime and method, stochastic variation in training determines whether the consensus threshold is crossed.\nAt this boundary, canary sensitivity is metric-dependent: token entropy fails to reach FDR significance in any seed ($p_{\\text{fdr}}{\\geq}0.086$), while ECE achieves significance in the two signal-positive seeds (s43, s44), identifying ECE as the more sensitive canary at the onset boundary.\nFigure~\\ref{fig:consensus_heatmap} visualizes this interaction across all method$\\times\\alpha$ combinations, revealing a clear block structure that separates correlation-based methods from causal methods as a function of regime.\n\n\n\\subsection{Case Study: Canary Prediction of Diversity Collapse}\n\\label{sec:case_study}\n\nHaving established method sensitivity (Claim~3) and regime dependence (Claim~2), we present the conditions under which canary prediction succeeds (Claim~1).\nWe scope this claim precisely: canary metrics predict \\emph{diversity collapse} (declining distinct-$n$), not model collapse broadly.\n\n\\paragraph{Full synthetic replacement ($\\alpha{=}1.0$, 4 seeds).}\nToken entropy exhibits robust Granger-precedence over diversity collapse metrics, confirmed by 4 independent statistical methods (permutation Granger power${=}1.0$), 4 seeds, and cross-architecture replication on GPT-2-medium (Tier~3 consensus; \\S\\ref{sec:discussion}).\nCross-correlation peaks at lag$={+}1$ with $|r|{>}0.99$ (cross-seed standard deviation: 0.0), establishing a consistent one-generation lead.\nFigure~\\ref{fig:metric_trajectory} illustrates the one-generation offset between token entropy decline and distinct-1 decline, visible across all four seeds.\nPermutation Granger tests (first-differenced) confirm all canary$\\to$diversity pairs as FDR-significant across all four seeds, including distinct-3 (Appendix~\\ref{app:distinct3}).\nReverse-direction tests (downstream$\\to$canary) find \\textbf{0/24} FDR-significant (all $p{>}0.49$), establishing complete directional asymmetry.\n\n\\begin{figure}[t]\n\\centering\n\\includegraphics[width=\\linewidth]{figures/metric_trajectory.pdf}\n\\caption{Metric trajectories across 10 generations at $\\alpha{=}1.0$ (Pythia-410M, 4~seeds shown as individual traces). Token entropy (top, blue) drops sharply at generation~1, while distinct-1 (bottom, red) begins declining at generations~2--3, establishing a visible one-generation canary lead. The consistent lag across all four seeds confirms that the temporal offset is robust rather than seed-specific. Shaded bands show the inter-seed range. This visual pattern corresponds to the statistical finding of 24/24 forward-direction and 0/24 reverse-direction FDR-significant pairs.}\n\\label{fig:metric_trajectory}\n\\end{figure}\n\n\\paragraph{High contamination ($\\alpha{=}0.75$, 3 seeds).}\nDirectional asymmetry is robustly confirmed across three independent seeds (permutation Granger: 19/24 forward pairs FDR-significant, 0/24 reverse).\nHowever, method-level consensus varies by seed (Tier~3 pairs: 4, 3, and 1 across seeds 42, 43, 44), placing $\\alpha{=}0.75$ squarely in the transitional detection regime.\nDifferenced cross-correlation finds 8/8 pairs significant in \\emph{both} forward and reverse directions at each seed, confirming that this method alone cannot distinguish genuine directional precedence from shared dynamics, a seed-level demonstration of Pitfall~1 (\\S\\ref{sec:pitfalls}).\n\n\\paragraph{ECE as a conditional secondary canary.}\nECE's forward consensus matches token entropy (both achieve PermG\\_fwd 6/8 across all conditions), but the reverse profile diverges sharply.\nAcross 7 validated conditions (3 architectures $\\times$ 2 scales $\\times$ 2 domains), token entropy reverse direction yields \\textbf{0/28} FDR-significant pairs (consistent unidirectional causality), while ECE reverse direction yields \\textbf{13/28} significant pairs, with 5/7 conditions containing at least one reverse-significant pair, all in the distinct$\\to$ECE direction.\nThis bidirectional coupling is the core reason ECE is classified as a secondary canary: ECE and diversity exhibit mutual feedback, and the interpretability of ECE's causal direction is correspondingly limited.\nThis result also instantiates Claim~3 (method sensitivity): when only forward significance is considered, the two canary types appear equivalent; incorporating reverse-direction tests separates them. The choice of analysis dimension alters the canary classification itself.\n\n\\paragraph{Failed canary candidates.}\nNot all metrics serve as canaries.\nEffective rank and Self-BLEU show no detectable lead-lag relationship with any downstream metric under any method or contamination level, and are excluded from the consensus framework.\nEffective rank behavior is model-dependent in an unexpected way: in Pythia-410M, effective rank decreases during collapse (representational contraction), whereas in GPT-2-medium at $\\alpha{=}0.25$ it \\emph{increases} from 2.37 to 8.48 (+258\\%).\nThe GPT-2-medium starting effective rank is near rank-2, suggesting the model's initial representation is already highly concentrated; iterative training disperses it rather than concentrating it further.\nThis opposite directionality across architectures precludes effective rank as a universal canary and motivates architecture-specific analysis (\\S\\ref{sec:discussion}).\n\n\\paragraph{Consensus filtering.}\nOf 16 canary$\\to$downstream pairs tested at $\\alpha{=}1.0$ (4 candidate canaries $\\times$ 4 downstream metrics), \\textbf{6 survive} the full consensus pipeline ($K{\\geq}4/5$ methods, $S{\\geq}3/4$ seeds): token entropy and ECE each predicting distinct-1 and distinct-2 (4 pairs; distinct-3 results in Appendix~\\ref{app:distinct3} bring the total to~6).\nThe 10 eliminated pairs comprise all canary$\\to$perplexity relationships (identified as spurious by the first-differencing diagnostic) and all effective-rank and Self-BLEU pairs (failed canaries).\nThe consensus framework reduces the naive 100\\% significance rate to the 37.5\\% rate supported by the most conservative individual methods (Figure~\\ref{fig:consensus_workflow}).\n\n\n\\subsection{Consensus Framework Validation}\n\\label{sec:consensus_validation}\n\nWe validate the consensus framework's calibration using built-in experimental controls.\n\n\\paragraph{False positive rate.}\nAt $\\alpha{=}0.0$ (three seeds, 24 total pairs), no synthetic data is injected; any detected lead-lag association is a false positive.\nIndividual-method FPR ranges from \\textbf{100\\%} (naive cross-correlation) through 37.5\\% (differenced) and 20.8\\% (threshold) down to \\textbf{0\\%} (permutation Granger: 0/24 across all three seeds).\nThe consensus framework at $K{\\geq}4/5$ achieves \\textbf{FPR${=}$0\\%} (0/24 pairs, three independent seeds), matching the best individual methods while providing robustness against miscalibration of any single method (Table~\\ref{tab:fpr_fnr}).\n\n\\paragraph{False negative rate.}\nAt $\\alpha{=}1.0$, we define 6 effect-size reference pairs (token entropy and ECE $\\to$ distinct-1/2/3) using criteria independent of the consensus methods: drift ratio ${\\geq}3\\times$ the $\\alpha{=}0.0$ baseline (entropy $11.93\\times$, ECE $73.12\\times$) and canary onset strictly preceding downstream onset by ${\\geq}1$ generation (\\S\\ref{sec:calibration}).\nFNR is calibrated on a 4-method panel (excluding differenced cross-correlation to avoid circularity): at $K{\\geq}3/4$, $S{\\geq}3/4$, all~6 are recovered (\\textbf{FNR${=}$0\\%}).\nToda--Yamamoto alone yields FNR${=}$33.3\\% (2/6), missing ECE$\\to$distinct-2 and ECE$\\to$distinct-3 due to Type~II errors at $T{=}10$.\nBoth FPR and FNR are validated on the joint method${\\times}$seed dimension (FPR: 3~seeds at $\\alpha{=}0.0$; FNR: 4~seeds at $\\alpha{=}1.0$), with cross-seed FPR variance negligible (all three seeds yield 0/8 robust pairs at $K{\\geq}4/5$).\nTable~\\ref{tab:fpr_fnr} reports the calibration results.\nTable~\\ref{tab:single-method-errors} complements this analysis by showing the practical consequences of relying on any single method: naive cross-correlation produces false positives at every $\\alpha$ including the null control, Toda--Yamamoto misses true signals at $\\alpha{=}0.75$, while the consensus at $K{\\geq}3$ yields the correct verdict across all five contamination levels.\n\n\\begin{table}[t]\n\\centering\n\\caption{Single-method inference errors across contamination levels.\nEach cell shows the verdict (significant or not) based on multi-seed majority ($\\geq$2/3 seeds with $\\geq$4/8 pairs significant).\n\\checkmark\\ = correct, \\textcolor{red}{FP} = false positive, \\textcolor{blue}{FN} = false negative, $\\circ$ = ambiguous ground truth (either verdict plausible).\nNaive XCorr produces false positives at \\emph{every} $\\alpha$ including the null control, while TY Granger misses true signals at $\\alpha{=}0.75$.\nOnly the multi-method consensus ($K{\\geq}3$) yields the correct verdict at all five contamination levels.}\n\\label{tab:single-method-errors}\n\\resizebox{\\columnwidth}{!}{%\n\\begin{tabular}{lccccc}\n\\toprule\n & \\multicolumn{2}{c}{\\textit{No signal}} & \\textit{Ambiguous} & \\multicolumn{2}{c}{\\textit{Causal signal}} \\\\\n\\cmidrule(lr){2-3} \\cmidrule(lr){4-4} \\cmidrule(lr){5-6}\nMethod & $\\alpha{=}0.00$ & $\\alpha{=}0.25$ & $\\alpha{=}0.50$ & $\\alpha{=}0.75$ & $\\alpha{=}1.00$ \\\\\n\\midrule\nNaive XCorr\n  & \\textcolor{red}{FP}{\\scriptsize\\ 8,7,8}\n  & \\textcolor{red}{FP}{\\scriptsize\\ 8,8}\n  & $\\circ${\\scriptsize\\ 8,8,8}\n  & \\checkmark{\\scriptsize\\ 8,8,8}\n  & \\checkmark{\\scriptsize\\ 8,8,8} \\\\\nDiff XCorr (FDR)\n  & \\checkmark{\\scriptsize\\ 4,3,3}\n  & \\checkmark{\\scriptsize\\ 3,1}\n  & $\\circ${\\scriptsize\\
